===== Module 2: The Respiratory and Autonomic Nervous Systems [_7758062_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758062_1?view=content&state=view
The autonomic nervous system is the portion of the nervous system that regulates our most important involuntary physiologic processes. Module 2 allows you to picture the drugs that work by affecting the Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) and the drugs that treat disorders of the Respiratory System.
Children:
Module overview
Chapter 2.1: Foundational information for understanding Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) Pharmacology
Chapter 2.2: Sympathomimetics (drugs that mimic the sympathetic nervous system)
Chapter 2.3: Sympatholytic Drugs (drugs that block the sympathetic nervous system)
Chapter 2.4: Pharmacology of the Parasympathetic Nervous System (PNS)
Chapter 2.5: Pharmacology of the Respiratory System

===== Module overview [_7758198_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758095_1?view=content&state=view
Module overview: Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) and the Respiratory system
In this module, we explore the medications of the Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) and the Respiratory system. These are very important topics.
This entire module contains a total of 2 hours 15 minutes of video lessons plus activities that reinforce your knowledge.

===== Activity: Watch this video introduction to Chapter 1 [_7758402_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758199_1?view=content&state=view
Video: Introduction to chapter 1
YouTube link.
PHAR2001_2_1_0_Introduction_to_Chapter
Objectives, PowerPoint and Notes
The objectives for this chapter include:
Describe the general properties of the Autonomic nervous system (ANS) (including the bodily functions it helps regulate, its two neuron nature, the neurotransmitters used, and the enzymes that degrade those neurotransmitters in the synaptic cleft).
Give the other names of a sympathomimetic drug (for instance, adrenergic agonist), a sympatholytic drug (for instance, an adrenergic antagonist or blocker), an acetylcholine agonist (for instance, a muscarinic drug) and an acetylcholine antagonist (for instance, an antimuscarinic drug)
Name the structures affected by the alpha 1, the beta 1 and the beta 2 receptors of the sympathetic nervous system, and for each of those structures note the effect of a 1. Sympathomimetic drug, and 2. Sympatholytic drug.
Compare the actions of the sympathetic and parasympathetic nervous systems on the heart, the pupils of the eyes, the entire gastrointestinal system including the amount of saliva that is produced, the lungs, and the urinary bladder.
The PowerPoint and Notes for this chapter are linked directly below.

https://youtu.be/up-DvC3JFtc

                                                              5
                                                                                                                        17/06/2025




          ANS pharmacology has one
          simple rule

         Drugs that affect the autonomic nervous
         system have one of four actions
               1. They can increase the sympathetic system
                  response
               2. They can increase the parasympathetic
                  system response
               3. They can decrease the sympathetic system
                  response
               4. They can decrease the parasympathetic
                  system response




17




          In medicine, there are always
          exceptions to the rule…
         Drugs that affect the autonomic nervous system have one of four
         actions
              1. They can increase the sympathetic system response
              2. They can increase the parasympathetic system response
              3. They can decrease the sympathetic system response
              4. They can decrease the parasympathetic system response

         EXCEPT:
            • Alpha 2 receptors (sympathetic system)
            • Central nicotinic receptors
              (parasympathetic system)
          LET’S IGNORE THE EXCEPTIONS UNTIL LATER
          (postpone readings until the appropriate time)

## ppt/slides/slide5.xml
Describe the general properties of the ANS including the bodily functions it helps regulate, its two neuron nature, the neurotransmitters used, and the enzymes that degrade those neurotransmitters in the synaptic cleft.
Give the other names of a sympathomimetic drug (for instance, adrenergic agonist), a sympatholytic drug (for instance, an adrenergic antagonist or blocker), an acetylcholine agonist (for instance, a muscarinic drug) and an acetylcholine antagonist (for instance, an antimuscarinic drug)
Name the structures affected by the alpha 1, the beta 1 and the beta 2 receptors of the sympathetic nervous system, and for each of those structures note the effect of a 1. Sympathomimetic drug, and 2. Sympatholytic drug.
Compare the actions of the sympathetic and parasympathetic nervous systems on the heart, the pupils of the eyes, the entire gastrointestinal system including the amount of saliva that is produced, the lungs, and the urinary bladder
.

## ppt/slides/slide17.xml
ANS pharmacology has one simple rule
Drugs that affect the autonomic nervous system have one of four actions
They can increase the sympathetic system response
They can increase the parasympathetic system response
They can decrease the sympathetic system response
They can decrease the parasympathetic system response

## ppt/slides/slide18.xml
In medicine, there are always exceptions to the rule…
Drugs that affect the autonomic nervous system have one of four actions
They can increase the sympathetic system response
They can increase the parasympathetic system response
They can decrease the sympathetic system response
They can decrease the parasympathetic system response
EXCEPT:
Alpha 2 receptors (sympathetic system)
Central nicotinic receptors (parasympathetic system) 
LET’S IGNORE THE EXCEPTIONS UNTIL LATER (postpone readings until the appropriate time)

===== Overview of the Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) [_7758403_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758200_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction to the nervous system
The various divisions of the nervous system can be very confusing. Worse yet, if the information is not fresh in your mind, it is impossible to understand the rest of material in this module. So let's engage in this quick review that places the autonomic nervous system (ANS) into the context of the other portions of the nervous system.
Video: Overview of Autonomic Nervous System
Watch this video, and perform the two drag and drop exercises below. Remember that the questions in the 30% quiz are derived from these activities so if you don't score well on your first attempt, try the activity again!
ADJUSTING SPEED OF VIDEO: Remember that you can listen to the reviews at a faster speed if you feel you already know the information.
It is a slightly different process to the videos from the last chapter,
where I put the material on YouTube. After the video starts, there is a Playback rate adjustment at the bottom centre portion of the video, that can be set at 0.5x, 1x, 1.4x. 1.6x and 2x. You can watch the video in YouTube
right here.

===== Divisions of the ANS [_7758201_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758201_1?view=content&state=view

Divisions of the ANS

===== Divisions of the ANS [_7758404_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758201_1?view=content&state=view
The two divisions of the ANS
The two divisions of the autonomic nervous system are the Sympathetic Nervous System (SNS) and the Parasympathetic Nervous System (PNS). The SNS and PNS have, for the most part, opposite effects because the autonomic nervous system helps maintain homeostasis. So, if our heart was beating too slow for the conditions at the time, our cells would start getting too little oxygen, lactic acid would start to accumulate and homeostasis would be disrupted. But that does not happen because the SNS comes to the rescue, sending out a bit of adrenaline and homeostasis is maintained.
Video: Divisions of the ANS
After watching this video and you should be able to answer the multiple choice question below.
These activities will make sure that you can apply logic to understanding the actions of the SNS and the PNS to our various organs.
You can

--- h5p_df9756db81ce39d8e8d9 ---
Dilation of the airways leading to more air intake

Increased heart rate and increased contractility of the heart to supply fresh blood at a rapid pace

Decreased salivation leading to dry mouth and decreased movement of the GIT leading to possible constipation if the response is long enough.

A constriction of the 'peripheral' blood vessels (like to the skin, to the GIT) leading to increased blood pressure.

Pupils become very dilated, leading to possible eye pain if the room is brightly lit.

High levels of glucose because we need instant energy

Constriction of the blood vessels to the big muscles that we need to run away

Finish ?

Retry ?

For the most part, we can predict what will happen with a sympathetic response if we take each organ and think about it logically. The exception to that is the effect of that response in the urinary system. With a fight or flight response, there are things relaxing and contracting all over the place, but the ultimate response is that "we need to flee not wee". There is an overall decrease in the ability to urinate. (But in some cases, you will hear about someone who did the opposite during a good fright, because of the complexity of the response at the urinary system.)
Other organs are much more predictable. Choose the response(s) that you would expect to find in a sympathetic nervous system response:

Untitled Multiple Choice

===== Overviewing drugs of the ANS [_7758202_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758202_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Overviewing drugs of the ANS

===== Overviewing drugs of the ANS [_7758405_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758202_1?view=content&state=view
Overview
In this lesson, we outline one simple rule (along with two exceptions to the rule) that allows you to easily understand the drugs of the ANS.
By watching this video and doing the associated activities, you will streamline your work involved in understanding of the drugs of the ANS.
Just a few terms that are extremely important at this point:
Sympathomimetics are drugs that mimic the sympathetic nervous system, like adrenaline and dopamine. They can also be called sympathetic nervous system agonists. While they often have a similar clinical outcome to the ParasympathoLYTIC drugs, THEY ARE NOT.
Sympatholytic drugs are those that BLOCK the sympathetic nervous system, like the beta blockers. They can also be called sympathetic nervous system antagonists. While they often have a similar clinical outcome to the ParasympathoMIMETIC drugs, THEY ARE NOT.
Parasympathomimetics are drugs that mimic the parasympathetic nervous system (the rest and digest system), like acetylcholine, although acetylcholine itself cannot be used as a drug because of its incredibly short half life. They can also be called parasympathetic nervous system agonists.
A parasympathoLYTIC drug is a drug that is against the parasympathetic nervous system. It blocks acetylcholine and is often also called an anticholinergic drug or an antimuscarinic drug.
Video: Drugs of the ANS
View this video on YouTube here.
PHAR2001_2_1_3_Overviewing_drugs_of_the_ANS

A drug gives a side effect of thirst. This would be a result
of a drug that increased the SNS or decreased the PNS

A drug is contraindicated in asthmatic clients. This would be likely with drugs that decreased the SNS or increased the PNS due to the constriction of the airways.

A drug has a side effect of constipation. The drug would most likely be one that increases the SNS or decreases the PNS.

A drug gives your client a racing heart. This would be likely with drugs that increase the SNS or decrease the PNS

A drug makes urination difficult. This would be likely with drugs that decrease the SNS or increase the PNS

We can predict the actions of drugs using the simple rule that we learned in this lesson. Choose the correct statement(s) from the following list:

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

You can predict the actions of adrenaline on various organs, as the previous activity just proved. But the adrenergic alpha 2 receptor is a receptor primarily aimed at controlling the sympathetic nervous system response and does the opposite of what we would expect in some organs. Therefore, we will be putting the drugs that primarily act on the alpha 2 receptor aside and not discuss those drugs in this module.

===== Selective receptors of the ANS [_7758406_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758203_1?view=content&state=view
Understanding receptors of the ANS
The concept of subtypes of the alpha1, 2, 3, and 4-adrenoceptor was first suggested in the mid 1980s and since then we have started to develop more and more selective medications. Yes, it made our work as students a bit more difficult but it really decreased the side effects from such medications. Now, we can affect the heart without affecting the salivation. We can affect the peripheral blood vessels without affecting the lungs.
It takes a bit of practice but you will start to understand the side effects of a medication simply by knowing which receptors it binds to after engaging with this lesson.
Video: Selective SNS receptors

PHAR2001_2_2_0_Introduction_to_Chapter_2_of_the_ANS
Objectives, PowerPoint and Notes
The objectives for this Chapter include:
Describe the actions, interactions and side effects of the following adrenergic agonists:
Adrenaline (non-selective agonist)
Phenylephrine (an alpha 1 selective agonist)
Dobutamine (a beta 1 adrenergic agonist)
Salbutamol (a beta 2 adrenergic agonist)
Dopamine
These medications will be taken again at a later time. It is most important right now to understand why the actions and side effects occur
The PowerPoint and Notes for this Chapter are directly below:
Links:
https://youtu.be/-OLMyciMbhE

5




                  Non-selective sympathomimetic
         • Specific drugs:
             • Adrenaline (know this drug)
             • Noradrenaline
             • Metaraminol
             • Ephedrine
             • Dopamine (know this drug)
             • Pseudoephedrine (Know; combined with antihistamines and analgesics)
         • These have limited use due to their effect on a wide
           range of organs, but are still used for treatment of
           hypotension and anaphylactic shock.
             • After intravenous administration of adrenaline, an Alpha-adrenergic
               antagonist should be administered locally to offset the vasoconstricting
               effects of adrenaline.

                          Non-selective sympathomimetic
            • Specific drugs:
                 • Adrenaline (know this drug)
                 • Noradrenaline
                 • Metaraminol
                 • Ephedrine
                 • Dopamine (know this drug)
                 • Pseudoephedrine (Know; combined with antihistamines and analgesics)
            • These have limited use due to their effect on a wide
              range of organs, but are still used for treatment of
              hypotension and anaphylactic shock.
                 • After intravenous administration of adrenaline, an Alpha-adrenergic
                   antagonist should be administered locally to offset the vasoconstricting
                   effects of adrenaline.

            ‘Selective’ adrenergic receptor stimulation
                  Subtypes of adrenergic receptors
                  • Alpha
                     • Alpha 1
                     • Alpha 2
                  • Beta
                     • Beta 1
                     • Beta2

                  While drugs that target these ‘selective’ receptors have more
                     selectivity, they still result in some stimulation to the
                           other receptors, especially in high doses.

                                                                                          7
                                                                                17/06/2025




                             Alpha 1 receptors
         Location of receptors:
            • ‘Peripheral’ blood vessels (including those in the nasal
              passages; large muscles do not have this constriction to
              ensure they get enough blood to move quickly)
            • Smooth muscles of the GIT (less effect than Beta 1
              stimulation)
            • Sphincters of the bladder
            • Radial muscles of the eye

          Given the location of the receptors, hypothesise the
          uses and side effects of Alpha 1 sympathomimetics




22




     Alpha 1 agonists: side effects

     Any sympathetic nervous system
     actions can be increased, but the most
     common adverse effects include:

       • Hypertension
       • Constipation
       • Urinary retention

       • (eye pain is possible)




23




                     Actions: Alpha 1 agonists
         • Nasal passages and eyes:
            • The constrictive effects of Alpha 1 adrenergic agonists can
              counteract the vasodilation that leads to nasal congestion and
              the ‘red eye’.
         • Systemically:
            • Increase in blood pressure (through a constriction of blood
              vessels).
         • Dilate the pupils
            • Allows as much light in as possible but can also lead to eye
              pain when too much light is being allowed in.
         • In minor surgery
            • Local administration constricts blood vessels for less bleeding
              and decreased systemic absorption of local anaesthesia


24




                                                                                        8
                                                                    17/06/2025




     Alpha 1 agonists

     (Most end in -ine, like epinephrine but that is not a formal
     suffix)


     Phenylephrine (know this one; used primarily as a
     decongestant, as an agent to dilate the pupils, and to
     increase blood pressure. )
     Midodrine (oral decongestant)
     Naphazoline (intranasal decongestant)
     Oxymetazoline (know this drug; intranasal decongestant)
     Xylometazoline (intranasal decongestant)


25




     Alpha 2 agonists

     This is the exception to the rule so ignore for now.


     Explanation: the Alpha 2 receptor exists to make sure
     that the adrenaline response does not result in so
     much stimulation that it can do harm. It is a ‘negative
     feedback’ type of receptor and does exactly opposite
     to that which one would expect.

     Autonomic Nervous System

                                                                            9

     Beta 1 adrenergic receptors

     Location
        • Heart
        • Git
        • Kidneys



     Given the location of the receptors, hypothesise the
     uses and side effects of Beta 1 agonists (selective Beta 1
     sympathomimetics)




28




              Actions: Beta 1 adrenergic agonists
          • Positive chronotropic effects (increased heart rate)
          • Positive inotropic effect (increase in the contractile force of the
            heart)
             • Know these terms – chrono=time (beats/min) inos=fibre (how
               much stretch
          • Therefore these drugs are useful in circulatory shock,
            hypotension and cardiac arrest
          • Adverse effects
             • GIT – constipation
             • Kidneys – decreased blood flow = increased systemic blood
               pressure via the renin angiotensin aldosterone mechanism
          • Example: Dobutamine (know this drug; used to treat acute but
            potentially reversible heart failure)


29




     Beta 2 receptors

     Location
        • Lung
        • Mast cells
        • Skeletal muscle



     Given the location of the receptors, hypothesise the
     uses and side effects of Beta 2 agonists (selective
     Beta 2 sympathomimetics)




30




                                                                                         10
                                                                           17/06/2025




                    Actions: Beta 2 agonists
       • Bronchodilation
       • Mast cell stabilisation
       • (Side effect: often associated with muscle tremor)

       • Used in chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, acute
         asthma and premature labour
       • Eformoterol, indacaterol, salmeterol
       • Salbutamol (know this drug; used for the relief of
         bronchospasm in conditions such as asthma and COPD

## ppt/slides/slide6.xml
Non-selective sympathomimetic 
Specific drugs:
Adrenaline (know this drug)
Noradrenaline
Metaraminol
Ephedrine
Dopamine (know this drug)
Pseudoephedrine (Know; combined with antihistamines and analgesics)
These have limited use due to their effect on a wide range of organs, but are still used for treatment of hypotension and anaphylactic shock. 
After intravenous administration of adrenaline, an Alpha-adrenergic antagonist should be administered locally to offset the vasoconstricting effects of adrenaline.

## ppt/slides/slide18.xml
Non-selective sympathomimetic 
Specific drugs:
Adrenaline (know this drug)
Noradrenaline
Metaraminol
Ephedrine
Dopamine (know this drug)
Pseudoephedrine (Know; combined with antihistamines and analgesics)
These have limited use due to their effect on a wide range of organs, but are still used for treatment of hypotension and anaphylactic shock. 
After intravenous administration of adrenaline, an Alpha-adrenergic antagonist should be administered locally to offset the vasoconstricting effects of adrenaline.

‘Selective’ adrenergic receptor stimulation
Subtypes of adrenergic receptors
Alpha
Alpha 1
Alpha 2
Beta
Beta 1 
Beta2
While drugs that target these ‘selective’ receptors have more selectivity, they still result in some stimulation to the       other receptors, especially in high doses.

## ppt/slides/slide22.xml
Alpha 1 receptors
Location of receptors:
‘Peripheral’ blood vessels (including those in the nasal passages; large muscles do not have this constriction to ensure they get enough blood to move quickly)
Smooth muscles of the GIT (less effect than Beta 1 stimulation)
Sphincters of the bladder
Radial muscles of the eye
 Given the location of the receptors, hypothesise the uses and side effects of Alpha 1 sympathomimetics

## ppt/slides/slide23.xml
Alpha 1 agonists: side effects
Any sympathetic nervous system actions can be increased, but the most common adverse effects include: 
Hypertension
Constipation 
Urinary retention
(eye pain is possible)

## ppt/slides/slide24.xml
Actions: Alpha 1 agonists
Nasal passages and eyes:
The constrictive effects of Alpha 1 adrenergic agonists can counteract the vasodilation that leads to nasal congestion and the ‘red eye’.
Systemically:
Increase in blood pressure (through a constriction of blood vessels).
Dilate the pupils
Allows as much light in as possible but can also lead to eye pain when too much light is being allowed in. 
In minor surgery	
Local administration constricts blood vessels for less bleeding and decreased systemic absorption of local anaesthesia

## ppt/slides/slide25.xml
Alpha 1 agonists
(Most end in -
ine
, like epinephrine but that is not a formal suffix)
Phenylephrine (know this one; used primarily as a decongestant, as an agent to dilate the pupils, and to increase blood pressure. )
Midodrine (oral decongestant)

Xylometazoline (intranasal decongestant)

## ppt/slides/slide26.xml
Alpha 2 agonists
This is the exception to the rule so ignore for now. 
Explanation: the Alpha 2 receptor exists to make sure that the adrenaline response does not result in so much stimulation that it can do harm. It is a ‘negative feedback’ type of receptor and does exactly opposite to that which one would expect.

[2.60] Welcome back to the lecture series on the medications of the autonomic Nervous System.
[8.16] In this portion of the lecture series, we're going to be talking about the adrenergic agonists.
[15.04] So in other words, the drugs that act like adrenaline and those adrenergic agonists can be selective or non-selective.
[27.76] If they're non-selective, they pretty much act like adrenaline on all of the receptors the alpha one, alpha two, beta one and beta two receptors.
[39.76] If they're selective, they can be selective for the alpha one receptor.
[46.32] And if they're selective for the alpha one receptor, they work mainly on the blood vessels, the urinary bladder and the prostate.
[54.72] They can be selective for the beta one receptor. And beta one is mainly found on the heart.
[62.64] So that would increase the contractility and increase the heart rate.
[69.16] And they can also be selective for the beta two receptor.
[74.08] And that'll be on mainly on the lungs. And that'll really open up the lungs and be good for people with asthma.
[83.00] So let's take a closer look at these medications.

===== Anaphylaxis and adrenaline [_7758409_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758206_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction to non-selective sympathomimetics
In this lesson, we begin our discussion of the non-selective
sympathomimetics
. Long name - what does that mean? Well, a sympathomimetic means that it is a drug that acts like adrenaline. It
mimics
the
sympathetic
nervous system.
Specifically in this lesson, we will get a good picture of what happens in anaphylactic shock, and why adrenaline is the main therapy and why antihistamines can also be of value.
Video: Anaphylaxis and adrenaline
Please pay close attention in this video - this is such an important topic that I have included 5 quick activities that reinforce your knowledge!
View this on YouTube.
PHAR2001_2_2_1_Anaphylaxis_and_Adrenaline

[41.44] and we need to know that those pertain to the drugs that act like adrenaline.

[55.36] an adrenergic agonist will do as they stimulate various individual organs.

[73.93] The first classification of adrenergic agonists that we'll be looking at in this lecture is going to be the non-selective sympathetic memetics.
[84.61] Recall what we mean by non-selective.
[88.09] Remember that we have different subsets of the adrenaline receptor the alpha one, alpha two, beta one and the beta two receptors,
[97.85] adrenaline and other non-selective adrenergic agonists will hit all of those receptors equally.
[105.21] So they'll have a profound action all the way through the body.
[109.73] But in most applications, we need to narrow that down.
[114.05] We need to try to be more selective so that we don't have as many adverse effects.
[122.01] Here is a list of drugs that will fall under the classification of non-selective adrenergic agonists,
[129.23] and the ones that I want you to know have the words know this drug after the name.
[135.67] One of the most important uses of adrenaline itself is in the treatment of anaphylaxis, or sometimes called anaphylactic shock.
[144.35] And as such, we need to know exactly what anaphylaxis is and how it's treated.
[150.99] And to begin with, as an overview, there's four types of hypersensitivity reactions termed type one, type two, type three and type four.
[161.95] The type one hypersensitivity reaction is also called a true allergic reaction.
[168.91] So if a drug causes a type two or 3 or 4 hypersensitivity reaction, that's not a true allergy.
[177.91] We would say that they have a hypersensitivity to the drug, because we try to restrict the use of the term allergy to a true allergic reaction.
[189.12] Another thing to note is that we can have a type one hypersensitivity reaction without having anaphylaxis or anaphylactic shock.
[198.12] For instance, allergic rhinitis, or sometimes called hay fever, is a local type one hypersensitivity reaction in the sinuses.
[208.52] One can also have other local allergic reactions like skin allergies.
[214.80] Adrenaline is not normally used for the local allergic reactions,
[219.72] but when we have a reaction that becomes systemic and starts affecting a number of different organs, that's termed anaphylaxis.
[229.32] And that's the point at which our adrenaline is going to be the primary treatment.
[235.32] The type one hypersensitivity reaction begins as the person is exposed to the foreign protein, called the allergen.
[243.00] It's normally a protein, but in some instances it's not.
[247.44] Common allergens include proteins in bee stings and peanuts, shellfish and other fish.
[254.81] Also penicillin and many other drugs and other foreign particles.
[259.77] On the first exposure to the allergen, the immune system determines that it's harmful,
[265.81] and it sets up a cascade of events that ends up with these memory T cells and the plasma cells.
[274.45] That's an important step, because on the first exposure to the allergen,
[278.69] the person may have some reactions to the allergen, but it's not generally going to proceed to anaphylaxis.
[286.25] What that first exposure has done is to ensure that the body can respond really, really quickly the next time the body is exposed to that allergen.
[298.17] So now that the person has been exposed to the allergen once and their immune system is armed and waiting upon the second exposure to the allergen,
[308.73] there's an immediate type one hypersensitivity reaction that results in this mature B cell,
[316.27] called a plasma cell, releasing these Y shaped proteins called immunoglobulins or immunoglobulin E.
[324.95] The immunoglobulins then insert the bottom portion of this Y shaped structure, this protein that bottom portion into the mast cell.
[335.39] And the basophils, those are similar cells.
[338.87] And both of those become activated when that binding of the immunoglobulin occurs.
[346.87] The biggest difference between those cells is that the mast cell is in the tissues like it does in the sinuses,
[354.99] while the basophils are one of the blood cells, they float around in the blood as that immunoglobulin attaches to the mast cells in the basophils.
[364.95] Those cells release what we call bags of granules.
[370.03] Those cells have pre-formed bags of granules that contain mediators of inflammation,
[379.80] and one of the most important mediators of inflammation that's released at that time is histamine.
[386.96] Histamine does a lot of important things, and we need to understand those things in order to understand anaphylaxis.
[396.44] Firstly it constricts the bronchi. It also dilates the blood vessels.
[401.84] And finally it increases the permeability of the blood vessels.
[406.84] So massive amounts of histamine are being released throughout the body.

Retry ?

There are four types of hypersensitivity reactions. Which of the following is termed "a true allergy", and can be successfully treated with antihistamines and/or adrenaline injection? (it is treated with adrenaline and antihistamines if it causes widespread anaphylaxis, or treated with antihistamines if a local reaction such as allergic rhinitis (hay fever).

Untitled Multiple Choice

Histamine dilates the bronchi

Histamine Dilates the blood vessels

Histamine makes the blood vessels more permeable, leading to oedema (locally or systemically, depending on how extensive the reaction is)

Histamine is released from mast cells (a local reaction) and basophils (a systemic reaction) in response to the allergen

Please choose each of the statements that are true about the histamine that is released from mast cells during a true allergy (a Hypersensitivity Type I reaction)

true

Adrenaline is called the physiological antagonist of histamine, because it has the exact opposite effect of histamine.

Untitled True/False Question

A colleague just had a needle stick injury with an EpiPen (adrenaline injector) into her finger. She should consider a follow up of phentolamine (an adrenaline antagonist) into that local area to prevent necrosis, because adrenaline is such a strong constrictor of blood vessels that she could loose the tip of her finger.

false

Once a person experiencing anaphylaxis administers adrenaline, they should be fine and can continue on with their day as normal.

===== Other non-selective adrenergic agonists [_7758207_1] =====

===== Other non-selective adrenergic agonists [_7758410_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758207_1?view=content&state=view
Adrenaline and pseudoephedrine
Aside from adrenaline itself, the non-selective adrenergic agonists also include pseudoephedrine, which is a very effective decongestant and dopamine, which is pretty much only used in acute heart failure where the client needs a lot of stimulation to the heart in order to survive. We briefly look at both of those in this lesson.
Video: Non selective adrenergic agonists
Watch this video and reinforce your knowledge with the multiple choice and T/F questions below.
View this video on YouTube.

[29.28] and this is the point at which a lot of students get confused, because the sympathetic mimetic drugs,
[36.80] and also the sympathetic drugs are both used for heart failure.
[42.72] And why is that? Well, there's a lot of different kinds of heart failure.
[48.56] Dopamine will be used in the hospital setting for somebody who's in cardiogenic shock, they just don't have enough blood being pumped by the heart.
[59.68] They need some stimulus to the heart. And then there's the congestive heart failure, which is your kind of normal heart failure,
[70.44] the kind of heart failure that can be treated for 20 and 30 years.
[75.48] And it's a chronic heart failure. It's a long term heart failure.
[79.96] And for those types of heart failure, we usually use the sympathetic drugs.

--- h5p_6838d740d48ded35a807 ---
It is an oral decongestant that is very similar to phenylephrine (another decongestant)

It causes a constriction of the ‘peripheral’ blood vessels, which is the reason it is effective but that can result in an increase in blood pressure

It can result in an increase in heart rate, as there is some cross over action on the Beta 1 receptor, especially in higher doses.

It may be associated with dilated pupils because it acts like adrenaline on the alpha 1 receptor

It is a strong stimulant

===== Selective alpha agonists [_7758208_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758208_1?view=content&state=view

Selective alpha agonists

===== Selective alpha agonists [_7758411_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758208_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction to selective alpha agonists
The alpha-adrenergic agents will bind to and act like adrenaline on the peripheral blood vessels, the radial muscles of the eyes that controls the diameter of the pupils, the smooth muscles of the GIT, the male prostate and the urinary sphincter. They act like adrenaline on those sites. So please
predict the actions of those agents before clicking onto this next video
to see if you are right.
Video: Selective alpha agonists
Please engage in the video and the two activities below. Remember that if things are ever going too slow or too fast for you, you can set the video play speed higher or lower.

--- h5p_7d4595886ba10822952d ---
Salbutamol is also known as Ventolin and is primarily
administered by metred inhaler

Salbutamol dilates the airways and stabilises the mast cells, and as such is good for asthmatic clients.

Dobutamine is a beta 1 agonist that increases heart rate and increases contractility and is mainly used in the hospital setting.

The fact that dobutamine has a chronotropic effect tells you that it increases the contractility of the heart.

===== Activity: watch this video introduction to the chapter [_7758414_1] =====

Video: Introduction to chapter 3
PHAR2001_2_3_0_Introduction_to_Chapter_3
Objectives, PowerPoint and Notes
The objectives for this chapter includes to describe the actions interactions and side effects of the following adrenergic
antagonists
(blockers):
Phentolamine and labetalol (non-selective antagonist)
Prazosin and doxazosin (an alpha 1 selective antagonist)
Metoprolol and atenolol (a beta 1 selective adrenergic antagonist)

    Describe the actions interactions and side effects of

         adrenergic antagonist)
          These medications will be taken again at a later
        time. It is most important right now to understand
              why the actions and side effects occur.

    Non-selective sympatholytic
    drugs
    Mostly used to treat hypertension
    Labetalol (know this drug; used to treat pre-eclampsia)

    Adverse effects
       • Postural hypotension
       • Blurred vision
       • Bronchospasm (dyspnoea; contraindicated in
         asthma)
       • Swollen ankles
       • Nasal congestion




7




              Pre-eclampsia can advance to life
                   threatening eclampsia




8




    Alpha antagonists

    Recall the location of receptors:
       • Blood vessels
       • Smooth muscles of the GIT
       • Radial muscles of the eye
       • Sphincters of the bladder

    If you block the adrenergic activity of these,
        • Vasodilation = decreased blood pressure
        • Increased GI motility and secretions
        • Pupil constriction
        • Increased micturition




9




                                                                      3
                                                        17/06/2025




      Non-selective Alpha antagonists

     Phentolamine (Regitine; know this drug)
     Used to:
        • Control hypertensive emergencies,
          cocaine overdose, and adrenaline
          overdose
        • Locally to increase blood flow for
          instance in the location of an injection of
          adrenaline
     Side effects:
        • Nasal congestion
        • Postural (orthostatic) hypotension

                     Selective adrenergic blockers

     PHAR2001          (Part 1: Alpha 1 blockers)



     Dr Ken Wojcikowski




11




     Alpha 1 antagonists

     Receptors in the blood vessels and prostate

     Drugs end in ‘…osin’ alfuzosin, prazosin…


     Treatment of
        • Hypertension
        • Benign prostatic hyperplasia

          • May result in hypotension!!

13




     Autonomic Nervous System
     Pharmacology Sympatholytics
                            Lesson 4:
                     Selective adrenergic blockers
     Introductory Pharmacology
     PHAR2001            (Part 2: Beta blockers)



     Dr Ken Wojcikowski




14




      Beta blockers




15




                                                             5
                                                              17/06/2025




     Beta antagonists (…olol drugs)
     Can be non-selective:
        • Propranolol (know this drug; first of this class,
          but crosses blood brain barrier)
        • Timolol (know this drug; treatment of choice in
          the patient with hypertension and angina)
        • Acebutolol… (other examples)

     Or selective for Beta 1 or Beta 2 receptors, but
     there are no clinical applications for Beta 2
     antagonists
     Selective Beta 1 antagonists were developed to
     reduce bronchospasm
        • Contraindicated for those with asthma, COPD,
          allergies



16




     Beta 1 antagonists
     The blockage of the beta 1 receptors in the heart
        • Decreased chronotropic effects
        • Decreased inotropic effects
        • Together, these have the effect of
           • stabilising the rhythm and
           • leading to decrease in arrhythmias,
           • decreased cardiac workload
           • decreased oxygen consumption
     The blockage of the beta 1 receptors in the
     kidneys
        • Renin- angiotensin-aldosterone mechanism
          decreased
        • Decreased blood pressure


17




     Beta 1 antagonists
     Metoprolol (lopressor; know this drug)
     Betaxolol, Atenolol

     Adverse reactions:
        • Dizziness
        • Bradycardia
        • Orthostatic hypotension
        • Bronchospasm
     Never stop this drug abruptly!




18




                                                                      6
                                                                                                                                                        17/06/2025




                 The renin angiotensin aldosterone system
                    increases water and salt retention
     http://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone_system.png/700px-Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone_system.png




 A. Rad (me), CC BY-SA 3.0
 <http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by
 -sa/3.0/>, via Wikimedia Commons



19




             There is no clinical use for
             Beta 2 antagonists in humans
                                                                                                            Why?

             Recall the location of the receptors:
                             • Lung
                             • Mast cells
                             • Skeletal muscle

                                   (These would constrict the bronchioles and
                                  make mast cells more sensitive, so there is no
                                     clinical application for this type of drug)

## ppt/slides/slide1.xml
Introductory Pharmacology PHAR2001
Third Part/Chapter of the Autonomic Nervous System (ANS) Pharmacology
Dr. Ken Wojcikowski

## ppt/slides/slide2.xml
Objectives
Describe the actions interactions and side effects of the following adrenergic antagonists:
Phentolamine and labetalol (non-selective antagonist)
Prazosin and doxazosin (an alpha 1 selective antagonist)
Metoprolol and atenolol (a beta 1 selective adrenergic antagonist)
These medications will be taken again at a later time. It is most important right now to understand why the actions and side effects occur.

Block

Prevent

## ppt/slides/slide7.xml
Non-selective sympatholytic drugs
Mostly used to treat hypertension
Labetalol (know this drug; used to treat pre-eclampsia)
Adverse effects
Postural hypotension 
Blurred vision
Bronchospasm (dyspnoea; contraindicated in asthma)
Swollen ankles
Nasal congestion

## ppt/slides/slide8.xml
Pre-eclampsia can advance to life threatening eclampsia

## ppt/slides/slide9.xml
Alpha antagonists 
Recall the location of receptors:
Blood vessels 
Smooth muscles of the GIT
Radial muscles of the eye
Sphincters of the bladder
If you block the adrenergic activity of these, 
Vasodilation = decreased blood pressure
Increased GI motility and secretions
Pupil constriction
Increased micturition

## ppt/slides/slide10.xml
 Non-selective 
Alpha
 antagonists
Phentolamine (
Regitine
; know this drug)
Used to: 
Control hypertensive emergencies,  cocaine overdose, and adrenaline overdose
Locally to increase blood flow for instance in the location of an injection of adrenaline
Side effects:
Nasal congestion
Postural (orthostatic) hypotension

## ppt/slides/slide17.xml
Beta 1 antagonists
The blockage of the beta 1 receptors in the heart 
Decreased chronotropic effects 
Decreased inotropic effects
Together, these have the effect of 
stabilising the rhythm and 
leading to decrease in arrhythmias, 
decreased cardiac workload 
decreased oxygen consumption
The blockage of the beta 1 receptors in the kidneys 
Renin- angiotensin-aldosterone mechanism decreased 
Decreased blood pressure

## ppt/slides/slide20.xml
There is no clinical use for Beta 2 antagonists in humans
Why?
Recall the location of the receptors:
Lung
Mast cells
Skeletal muscle
(These would constrict the bronchioles and make mast cells more sensitive, so there is no clinical application for this type of drug)

===== Understanding sympatholytics [_7758212_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758212_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Understanding sympatholytics

===== Understanding sympatholytics [_7758415_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758212_1?view=content&state=view
Introduction to sympatholytic drugs
Sympatholytic drugs are drugs that block the sympathetic nervous system. Other names for the drugs include sympathetic nervous system antagonists, adrenergic antagonists, adrenergic blocking agents, there are many selective agents like alpha-blockers and beta-blockers. These are NOT the same as a parasympathomimetic agent but they do allow the parasympathetic rest and digest system to DOMINATE.
So, predict what would happen with a sympatholytic drug
.
Video: Sympatholytic agents
Please engage in these activities that will help you understand subsequent material about drugs that act against the sympathetic nervous system.
Watch this on YouTube here.

[21.12] The sympathetic drugs, the ones that act against the sympathetic nervous system are antagonising the effects of adrenaline,
[31.72] and therefore it's not going to be exactly like a parasympathetic mimetic or in other words, something that mimics the rest and digest system.
[42.72] But it's going to be pretty close. These medications will allow the parasympathetic nervous system to dominate.
[51.04] So let's predict what's going to happen to the pupils of the eyes in a fight or flight response.
[56.56] Our pupils are dilated to allow as much light in as possible.
[61.40] What is the medication that antagonises that action going to do?
[66.52] Well, there's going to be a lack of dilation. So basically a constriction of the pupils.
[72.17] And along with that there may be some blurring of the vision.
[76.25] Let's take a look at the heart. What would a sympathetic lytic drug do to the heart.
[83.61] It's going to work against that speeding up of the heart.
[87.01] So it's going to slow down the heart and decrease the contractility.
[92.29] A lot of our chronic heart problems wind up with compensatory mechanisms of sending out too much adrenaline to speed up the heart.
[101.45] And beta blockers are going to be valuable medications to reverse that, to slow that heart back down.
[109.73] And what is a sympathetic lytic drug going to do to the lungs?
[115.09] Remember, adrenaline relaxes the lungs opens up the bronchi.
[119.77] Without that relaxation the parasympathetic nervous systems constriction is going to dominate.
[126.17] So that's one of the primary problems with the what we call non-selective adrenergic blockers.
[134.33] Those drugs are going to block that beta two receptor and be contraindicated in asthma and other lung disorders.
[143.82] Let's take a look at the GI tract. Normally in a fight or flight response.
[147.78] Our git shuts down and now we're blocking that action.
[152.30] So we're going to allow the parasympathetic nervous system the rest in digest system to dominate.
[158.54] And therefore motility. And most secretions in the GI are going to increase.
[164.78] And if we antagonise those constricting effects on the blood vessels, it's in effect going to be like dilating the blood vessels.
[174.02] And just think about that. What's a possible side effect of that same exact action?
[180.74] Yes. If we relax the blood vessels too much, we could actually give the person hypertension.
[187.30] The main mechanism by which adrenergic antagonists work is by blocking the receptors on the postsynaptic neurone,
[195.10] although there's other mechanisms which will mention at the time that we present those medications.

They dilate the pupils of the eye

They increase salivation and all activity in the GIT

They constrict the bronchi.

They constrict the urinary sphincters making it more difficult to urinate

[78.89] and the relaxation of the blood vessels, that's going to be an effective antihypertensive agent.

Dyspnoea due to blocking the beta 2 receptor on the airways which normally open up the airways.

Nasal congestion due to the blocking of the alpha 1 receptor on the blood vessels in the sinuses, essentially doing the opposite thing that decongestants do.

A racing heart from partially blocking the Beta 2 receptor

Ankle oedema due to partially blocking the Beta 1 receptor

Retry ?

Labetalol is a valuable antihypertensive agent that is frequently used in women with pregnancy-induced hypertension, including pre-eclampsia. It is a pregnancy category C drug, which means that it could possibly cause harm to the foetus but if pre-eclampsia advances to eclampsia, it becomes a life threatening condition for both mother and child. It has a lot of side effects, and if you don't remember those, you can figure it out by the fact that it is non-selective, moderately blocking the alpha 1 and beta 2 receptors and partially blocking the beta 1 receptor. Which of the following is/are common side effect(s) of labetalol (and the correct reason for this effect)

Untitled Multiple Choice

Phentolamine

Labetalol

Oxymetazoline

Because of the profound vasoconstrictive effects of adrenaline, a needle stick injury with an EpiPen should be treated with which drug to ensure the finger does not become necrotic from lack of blood?

true

Labetalol can be considered a non-selective alpha blocker or a non-selective beta blocker owing to the fact that it has cross over effects onto both Alpha 1 and Beta 1 receptors. This gets confusing, as Beta 1 blockers will normally end in the suffix OLOL. The thing to remember about labetalol is the fact that it blocks the action of adrenaline on the peripheral blood vessels, thus, in effect, dilating them and blocks the beta 1 receptors on the receptors of the heart, thus decreasing heart rate and contractility. This combination appears to be especially effective in treating eclampsia, which involves increased blood pressure and proteinuria during pregnancy.

===== Beta blockers [_7758215_1] =====

Beta blockers

===== Beta blockers [_7758418_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758215_1?view=content&state=view
Overview of beta blockers
Beta blockers had been the primary therapy for hypertension, but ACE inhibitors, angiotensin II receptor antagonists, calcium-channel blockers, and thiazide diuretics are generally preferred over beta blockers for the treatment of hypertension in the absence of co-morbidities. Nonetheless, beta blockers are still an important medication for hypertension, especially if it involves
congestive heart failure
, the type of heart failure that is chronic (long term) and can be treated for 20 years or longer.

The first beta blockers that came out (the first generation beta blockers) were non-selective. They blocked both the beta 1 and the beta 2 receptors. When the selective beta blockers came out, they were selective, hitting 'only' the beta 1 receptor. The major advantage of the second generation beta blockers over the first generation ones is that fact that they do not cause:

Untitled Multiple Choice

false

In general, the term "first generation" means that it is non-selective, while the term 'second generation' means that it is selective.

Untitled True/False Question

true

Since Congestive Heart Failure (CHF) can be successfully treated for 20, 30 or more years, with a combination of Beta blockers and other medications, it is important to be sensitive about the way in which we explain the condition.

[0.88] Okay. So that was the adrenergic antagonists. And they can be either selective or non-selective.
[10.56] The non-selective one that I want you to remember is Fenton. And the selective ones include the alpha one selective antagonist.
[20.56] And those are drugs like the Ossian drugs, the appraisal, sin and sin.
[27.56] And they can also be selective for the beta one receptor as well.
[33.24] And those drugs are drugs like the metoprolol and atenolol.
[39.12] Now, it's interesting, isn't it, because both the beta one agonists and the beta one antagonists are used in heart failure.
[49.32] Strange, isn't it, because they're opposite drugs? Well, they're actually opposite conditions.
[54.84] Because in acute heart failure, the heart of the of the person is is simply not getting that impulse to really beat as hard as it needs to.
[67.99] It needs some stimulation, and it needs to increase that heart rate and increase the contractility.
[76.39] And in the chronic heart failure, what happens is that there's a lot of things that are going to increase the workload of the heart,
[86.91] and what we need to do is just say, well, give him something to chill.
[92.19] That heart needs to chill. And that's why we are using two completely opposite kind of drugs for quote unquote, heart failure.

Video: Introduction to chapter 4

PHAR2001_2_4_0_Introduction_to_Chapter_4
Objectives, PowerPoint and Notes
The objectives for Chapter 4 include:
Explain the difference between direct-acting and indirect-acting Parasympathomimetic drugs
Know terms used to describe the parasympathomimetic drugs, for instance, muscarinic drugs, cholinergic agents, acetylcholine agonists.
Describe the actions interactions and side effects of the:
Direct acting cholinergic agonists bethanechol and pilocarpine
Indirect acting cholinergic agonists neostigmine and donepezil
The antimuscarinic agents, using atropine as an example.

## ppt/slides/slide16.xml
Cholinergic agents
Direct acting       or      Indirect acting
Can be:
Nicotinic
Muscarinic (5 subsets)
M1 brain (decreased in Alzheimer’s disease)
M2 heart (cause decreased rate and force of heart beat)
M3 relaxation of GIT, lacrimal secretion, pupil constriction, increased micturition and defecation, bronchoconstriction 
M4 and M5 (not elucidated)

## ppt/slides/slide22.xml
Cholinergic agonists
Direct acting non-selective agonists include:
Pilocarpine 
(know this drug; used to treat glaucoma. While this is a non-selective agonist, pilocarpine is administered topically and has little systemic absorption, therefore the actions are fairly limited to the m3 receptors in the eyes)
Bethanechol
 (know this drug; previously used for non-obstructive urinary retention. Adverse effects: bradycardia, hypotension, diarrhoea, all consistent with parasympathetic stimulation.

## ppt/slides/slide23.xml
Direct acting cholinergic agonists
Indications include non-obstructive postoperative and postpartum urinary retention
Topically in eye to reduce intraocular pressure of glaucoma, mydriasis
Adverse effects
Extreme caution, even if used in eye
Bradycardia, 
Hypotension, 
Nausea, vomiting, 
Diarrhoea, dehydration
Bronchoconstriction

## ppt/slides/slide31.xml
Advanced Alzheimer’s disease
Donepezil
 (Aricept; know this drug) 
Reversible acetylcholinesterase inhibitor that is 100% bioavailable and crosses the BB barrier
Two clinical trials indicate it may be effective for moderate to severe Alzheimer’s disease
Should be used with caution in patients with cardiac disease, cardiac conduction disturbances, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, asthma
Patients with gastrointestinal disorders should use caution because nausea or vomiting may occur.

## ppt/slides/slide34.xml
Many of our medications inadvertently bind to and block the acetylcholine muscarinic receptor

## ppt/slides/slide35.xml
Anticholinergic agents allow the sympathetic nervous system to dominate

## ppt/slides/slide36.xml
Muscarinic antagonists
Include 
Atropine (know this drug)
Hyoscine
Hyoscamine
Cyclopentolate
Indications:
Antispasmodics in GIT spasm, 
Counterbalance bradycardia
Antiemetic
Antiasthma agent
Treatment of urinary frequency

## ppt/slides/slide37.xml
Deadly nightshade contains atropine that is an anticholinergic agent

## ppt/slides/slide38.xml
This is a common mnemonic for anticholinergic overdose
To describe the physiologic manifestations of atropine overdose: 
“Hot as a hare, blind as a bat, dry as a bone, red as a beet, and mad as a hatter“ describing 
warm, dry skin from decreased sweating, 
blurry vision, decreased lacrimation, 
vasodilation, and 
central nervous system effects on muscarinic receptors
This set of symptoms is known as anticholinergic toxidrome, and may also be caused by other drugs with anticholinergic effects, such as hyoscine hydrobromide (scopolamine), diphenhydramine, phenothiazine antipsychotics and benztropine

===== Understanding acetylcholine [_7758218_1] =====

Understanding acetylcholine

===== Understanding acetylcholine [_7758421_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758218_1?view=content&state=view
Learning about acetlycholine
Recall that the neurotransmitter that is used at the neuromuscular junction that results in a contraction of the muscle is acetylcholine. And now we are saying that the neurotransmitter that works at the effector (at each of the organs of the ANS) is acetylcholine.
So how is it that we can have medications that affect our organs without affecting muscle movement?
In this lesson, you will learn the answer to that question, and also learn the different names used for Parasympathetic Nervous System (PNS) drugs, and what their general effect will be if they cross into the brain.
Video: Understanding acetylcholine
You have to be curious about these beautiful mushrooms.
In the next lesson, you find out what will happen if you served these mushrooms up to your dinner guests. ...but you won't fully understand unless you watch this video! So click onto the play button and then engage in the four activities below.
YouTube link.
PHAR2001_2_4_1_Understanding_acetylcholine
Activity: Test your knowledge

--- h5p_5652a314874f045816ac ---
The acetylcholine that is released by the motor nerves at the neuromuscular junction binds to NICOTINIC receptors. (In other words, the neurotransmitter that moves your muscles binds to nicotinic receptors)

The acetylcholine that is released at the effector in the parasympathetic nervous system (PNS), binds to MUSCARINIC receptors. (In other words, the main receptors for Acetylcholine in the PNS are the muscarinic receptors.)

When we give a drug that affects the Parasympathetic Nervous System, it always affects muscle movement.

Which of the following is NOT a commonly used term for a Parasympathetic Nervous System (PNS) antagonist?

true

Some of our medications that are helpful in Alzheimer's disease increase the acetylcholine in the brain.

Untitled True/False Question

false

Anticholinergic agents that break the blood brain barrier (go into the brain) have no effect on memory or cognition

Retry ?

The poisonous, pretty mushrooms that were seen in this lesson contain the alkaloid MUSCARINE, which binds to the acetylcholine MUSCARINIC receptors in the PNS, and acts like an agonist. (it acts like acetylcholine)  As such they would cause which of the following?

Untitled Multiple Choice

true

Direct acting cholinergic drugs of the PNS act like acetylcholine on the muscarinic receptors. Indirect acting cholinergic drugs inhibit the enzyme acetylcholinesterase, that breaks down acetylcholine in the synaptic cleft, allowing the actual nerurotransmitter acetylcholine to remain in the synaptic cleft longer.

===== Direct vs indirect cholinergic agents [_7758423_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758220_1?view=content&state=view
Differences between direct and indirect cholinergic agents
For the most part, our ANS medications are receptor agonists and antagonists. But one notable exception is the indirect acting cholinergic agents that are enzymatic inhibitors. They act on and inhibit the enzyme that degrades acetylcholine. Let's take a look at the differences between direct and indirect cholinergic agents.

[175.77] So what's it going to do to the neuromuscular junction that uses the nicotinic receptors.
[183.89] Yes. There's going to be more available acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction as well.
[191.17] And just to give you an idea of how powerful these drugs can be.
[195.04] There's several nerve gases that work to irreversibly inhibit that same enzyme.
[201.96] Imagine irreversibly inhibiting the enzyme that breaks down the acetylcholine on the respiratory muscles.
[208.76] That would result in constant contraction, and the person would die of respiratory distress fairly quickly.
[215.32] And that was nerve gas that irreversibly inhibited that enzyme.
[221.00] Now our medications are going to affect that enzyme.
[224.72] But it's first of all not going to be irreversible. And secondly, there's a much smaller dose.
[231.00] So while we're not going to expect the person to die of respiratory distress, there is going to be some effect on the muscles.
[239.32] There is going to be some excessive contraction.
[242.44] And therefore one of the side effects of the medications, the acetylcholine esterase inhibitors, is going to be muscle cramps.

===== Indirect acting acetylcholine agonists [_7758221_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758221_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Indirect acting acetylcholine agonists

===== Indirect acting acetylcholine agonists [_7758424_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758221_1?view=content&state=view
Understanding Indirect acting acetylcholine agonists
The most common type of nerve gas irreversibly inhibits the enzyme acetylcholinesterase. Think about what that would do. There would be huge amounts of acetylcholine in the synaptic cleft because it is not degrading. But where would that excess be? In the MUSCLES, meaning there would be constant contraction - how can you breath if the diaphram is not able to relax? In the HEART, meaning there would be a profound bradycardia (decreased heart rate). In the GIT, giving excess salivation, cramping and diarrhoea. Now lets back up a bit and think about when a reversible version of that could be useful.
Video: Indirect acting acetylcholine agonists
Engage in this video and multiple choice question to understand the two different kinds of acetylcholine agonists.

Donepezil is an indirect acetylcholine agonist that can cross into the brain and as such is good for Alzheimer's disease.

Donepezil.

Retry ?

Myasthenia gravis is an autoimmune disorder in which the client’s own immune system degrades the nicotinic acetylcholine receptors in the muscles. In order to get more acetylcholine in the neuromuscular junction, we cannot selectively hit the nicotinic receptors, so we use a different approach.
Fistly, we administer an indirect cholinergic drug like neostigmine. That inhibits the enzyme that degrades acetylecholine and results in lots of acetylcholine. But it does it in the muscles AND in the PNS, yielding a lot of PNS side effects.
Then we take an acetylcholine muscarinic antagonist like ________ to decrease the PNS side effects.  Viola, we have more acetylcholine in the muscles and the normal amount in the PNS.

[13.44] And right now we're going to take a look at the anticholinergic agents.
[19.80] Other terms for anticholinergics include the parasympathetic clitics muscarinic antagonists and the anti muscarinic agents.
[31.00] There are a couple things to keep in mind as we begin to discuss the anticholinergic agents.
[36.92] Firstly, a lot of drugs inadvertently bind to the acetylcholine receptors and therefore have anticholinergic actions.
[47.12] For instance, the first generation antihistamines have variable affinity to the acetylcholine receptor,
[54.52] and as such they have some anticholinergic actions.
[59.56] And if you can start to hear the words anticholinergic actions and associate them with the effects of anticholinergic drugs,
[69.88] you can immediately identify some of the side effects that may be seen with a medication that has those anticholinergic actions.
[80.48] Let's give it a try. Now, what would you expect?
[83.48] An anticholinergic agent or a drug that inadvertently has anticholinergic effects to do at the level of the pupil of the eyes,
[93.68] the heart, the blood vessels, the gibt including the mouth and the urinary bladder?
[101.56] Well, at the level of the pupils, it's going to allow the sympathetic nervous system to dominate,
[107.68] dilating the pupils, and in some cases that's going to give eye pain.
[112.40] The sympathetic nervous system would also dominate at the level of the heart, speeding up the heart and increasing the contractility,
[120.97] and at the blood vessels there would be less relaxation, or in other words,
[126.21] the sympathetic nervous system would dominate and that would possibly result in an increase in blood pressure.
[133.85] The effect on the mouth would be a dry mouth. And think about that.
[137.77] A lot of medications cause dry mouth, and a lot of those medications are doing it because of the fact that they have anticholinergic actions.
[149.53] The activity in other portions of the GI would be decreased as well, possibly leading to constipation.
[156.21] And also there's a constriction of the sphincters of the bladder.
[160.05] And therefore it's going to possibly result in urinary retention.
[165.45] So a difficulty in urination. So those were the peripheral effects of an anticholinergic agent.
[173.57] And if a drug inadvertently has anticholinergic actions it's going to result in some of those side effects.
[180.89] But just a note about that. Usually the anticholinergic actions of drugs that aren't actually anticholinergic
[188.53] drugs are fairly subtle because the binding of the medication is fairly weak.
[194.81] So a person on that medication may report no side effects, while another one might say that they got a dry mouth but nothing else,
[203.77] and then other patients would have to be taken off the medication completely because they have really profound side effects.
[211.57] It is really patient specific as to the amount of side effects that would be experienced.
[218.57] The other thing to remember about the anticholinergic agents relates to the drug's ability to break through the blood brain barrier.
[227.97] Remember, our best treatment for dementia at this time includes increasing the amount of acetylcholine in the brain.
[236.65] So what do you think would happen if we decreased the acetylcholine in the brain.
[243.17] Indeed, the anticholinergic agents that break through the blood brain barrier are associated with confusion and drowsiness,
[252.17] and oftentimes an increased risk of developing Alzheimer's disease.
[258.45] So if you have that picture in your mind, we can go on to the most important anticholinergic agent that I'd like you to remember, which is atropine.
[269.37] If you think those mushrooms were bad, wait until you cook up some of the foliage and berries from this plant.
[276.49] This is a deadly nightshade plant, and it's considered to be very toxic because it contains the alkaloids atropine, scopolamine, and diamine.
[289.13] And all of those are anticholinergic agents that we still use as drugs, but we use them in purified forms and in a dose that's proven to be safe.
[300.14] Atropine was first isolated back in the 1830s, and it's still a very important medication.
[306.34] It's used topically to dilate the pupils. It's also used intravenously to reverse the poisoning from nerve gas.
[315.54] And it's also the medication that your dinner guests got to reverse the poisoning from those muscarinic mushrooms.
[326.10] And finally, it's used in some types of acute heart failure, where we aim to increase the heart rate and increase the contractility.
[336.70] It's going to help you understand the anticholinergic agent if you spend some time on this slide here,
[344.06] remembering the physiologic manifestations of atropine overdose.

In this final chapter of Module 2, we explore the respiratory system pharmacology. Our objectives for this chapter include the following:
Briefly describe the structure and function of the lungs including the immune system response in the lungs
Briefly describe the pathophysiology of allergic rhinitis and asthma identifying and describing the actions of common pharmacological agents used in their treatment
Compare the difference between mucolytic agents and expectorants, citing examples of each.

           Structure of the Respiratory System

    • Consists of the air passages and the lungs
    • Divided into two parts by function:
      • Conducting airways, through which air moves
        as it passes between the atmosphere and the
        lungs
      • Respiratory tissues of the lungs, where gas
        exchange takes place

                      Lung Circulation Involves
                      Two Separate Circulations

                                                              3
                                                    17/06/2025




10




                                      Ventilation

      Bulk flow of air
      Disorders that obstruct
      ventilation are called
      obstructive disorders
       Asthma, COPD, others

      Function
       Moves air out of the lungs but
        does not participate in gas
        exchange




11




12




                                                            4
                                                                     17/06/2025




                         External Respiration
                  (Also referred to as gas exchange)
     • Exchange of gases from an
       area of high concentration
       to an area of lower
       concentration
     • Takes place in the terminal
       respiratory bronchioles and
       the alveolar ducts and sacs
     • Can be reduced with
       inflammation, fibrosis, or
       decreased area



13




        How can pharmaceutical
             agents help?
     • Fighting infection
         • Antibiotics, antifungal agents
     • Preventing excess mucus production
     • Decreasing cough
     • Expelling mucus
     • Promoting ventilation or ‘bulk flow of air’
        • Promote airway adequacy by dilating airways or reducing
          excess mucus
     • Reducing inflammation
        • Inflammation can affect both ventilation (bulk flow) and
          respiration (exchange of gasses).

     kjhkjhlkdfs

                        Medications of the Respiratory

       PHAR2001_2_5_1
                                    Lesson 2
                         Antitussives, expectorants and
                                mucolytic agents




15




                                                                             5
                                                                                            17/06/2025




          Antitussives (suppress cough reflex)

     Cough suppressants
       • For Non-productive cough only
          • Depress the cough reflex located in the medullary cough centre (centrally
            acting)
          • Codeine (this is an opioid narcotic),
          • Dextromethorphan (this is a morphine derivative but not considered an opioid)




16




          Antitussives (suppress cough reflex)
     Cough suppressants

       • Codeine: Adverse effects:
          • Drowsiness, sedation (CNS depression), respiratory depression
       • Dextromethorphan: Adverse effects:
          • Allergic reactions, GIT complaints

       • Contraindications for codeine:
          • Pregnancy, young children, especially under 2

       • Dextromethorphan is a pregnancy category A drug




17




                                 Expectorants
          • Stimulate mucus secretion

          • Reduce the viscosity of thick tenacious

          • Guaifenesin (in Benadryl) PO

          • May result in some GI symptoms




18




                                                                                                    6
                                                                                 17/06/2025




                               Mucolytic agents
             • For productive coughs
                • Chronic bronchitis, pneumonia, cystic fibrosis
             • Decreases mucus viscosity/ liquefies secretions
                • Facilitates airway and secretion clearance
             • Acetylcysteine (know this drug)
                • Breaks down mucoproteins
                • Acetylcysteine is also the antidote for paracetamol toxicity
             • Dornase alfa
                • Breaks down DNA from proteins (specifically for cystic
                  fibrosis)
             • Adverse effects
                • Pharyngitis, GI disturbances

     kjhkjhlkdfs

                        Decongestants and Antihistamines




20




      In allergic rhinitis,
     histamine dilates the
     blood vessels of the
            sinuses.

        Decongestants
     constrict those blood
      vessels by acting on
          the alpha 1
     adrenergic receptors.




21




                                                                                         7
                                                                    17/06/2025




          The sinuses
     Normally, the sinuses are
     empty except for a thin
     layer of mucus.

     We probably have sinuses
     to humidify the air




22




                                  Decongestants
     • Decongestants
       • Help stop ‘runny nose’
       • Alpha agonists (act like adrenaline on the
         alpha 1 receptors)
          • Vasoconstriction = decrease in secretions,
            reduction in swelling of mucus membranes
            and oedema , opening of nasal passage

                                           Constrict the blood
                                          vessels that had been
                                           dilated by histamine




23




                                  Decongestants

       • Nasal sprays
          • Oxymetazoline (Drixine)
       • Oral medications
          • Pseudoephedrine (sale is restricted)
          • Phenylephrine (no better than placebo in most trials)

       • Adverse effects
          • Rebound congestion (!: Don’t use long-term)
          • Hypertension (other blood vessels are constricted)
          • Insomnia




24




                                                                            8
                                                                                17/06/2025




           Antihistamines (H1 antagonists)
               Discussed in the tutorial
     • H1 antagonists (NOT the H2 antagonists in gut)




                                                        Help stop this entire
                                                              cascade




25




           Antihistamines (H1 antagonists)
               Discussed in the tutorial
     • H1 are first generation (cross BBB; Dimenhydrinate)
       • Side effects include drowsiness and decreased motion sickness
          • These are often used for their ‘side effects’
       • These are associated with higher risk of Alzheimer’s disease
     • H1 can be second generation
       • do not cross BBB to the same degree, so less drowsiness
       • Loratadine and fexofenadine
     • Decrease the vasodilation effects of histamine
     • Antihistamines have varying amounts of secondary
       antimuscarinic actions
       • This is usually more profound with the first generation
         medications: dry mouth, difficulty urinating, eye pain

                        The histamine receptors

28




              Ventilation (obstructive) disorders
             • COPD (emphysema and chronic bronchitis)
                • Other disorders chronically obstruct but are not COPD


             • Chronic bronchitis (only)

             • Emphysema (only)

             • Asthma (10% of Australians have asthma)

             • Cystic fibrosis (a fairly common genetic disorder)

      Decreasing the lumen’s diameter by ½ = 1/16th the flow of air

30




                                                                                 10
                                                                                   17/06/2025




                                     Asthma
     • A condition characterised by:
        • Bronchospasm
        • Inflammation
        • Hyperactive airways
           • Mucus hyper secretion
           • Oedema
     • Extrinsic
        • Allergens
     • Intrinsic
        • Non-allergic: anxiety, exercise

         It may be best to consider an asthmatic to have a cup. Everything that
          irritates THEIR asthma fills up the cup. When the cup overflows, they
                                have an asthmatic attack.
         Try to get them to eliminate anything that contributes to their asthma.

                                                                                          11
                                                           17/06/2025
