     kjhkjhlkdfs

                        Medications of the Respiratory
       PHAR2001_2_5_1
                                  System:
                                     Lesson 5
                         Treatment of Asthma (and other
                              ventilation disorders)




35




                           Treatment of Asthma
     • Antihistamines (PO; oral; efficacy is questioned)
     • Bronchodilators (MDI; metred-dose inhaler)
        • Beta 2 agonists
        • Anticholinergic drugs
        • Others, for instance xanthines (oral)
     • Corticosteroids (MDI)
     • Leukotriene inhibitors (PO)
     • Corticosteroids (PO)




36




                                                                  12
                                                                                       17/06/2025




                   Antihistamines in asthma
     • Antihistamines
       • Block the release of histamine
          • Reduces one mediator in the inflammatory and allergic response
          • Limited use in asthma because the mast cell that releases histamine also
            releases other stronger mediators of inflammation and the asthmatic is
            normally more sensitive to those. But some asthmatics respond.
       • Adverse effects mainly seen with first generation antihistimines
         (the ones that cause drowsiness)
          • Anticholinergic effects if using first generation: don’t!
          • Dry mouth and drowsiness if using first generation: don’t!
          • CNS depressants if using first generation: don’t!
       • Use second generation antihistamines: Loratadine and
         fexofenadine and discontinue if no effect.




37




                    Bronchodilators: inhaled

     Dilate the airways, either mimicking the SNS (fight or flight) or
               blocking the PNS (rest and digest) in lungs




38




                    Bronchodilators: inhaled
     • Beta 2 agonist (mimics the adrenaline on the Beta 2
       receptor on lungs)
       • Beta 2 receptors are located on the smooth muscle of
         blood vessels, bronchi and MAST cells

       • Adverse effects
          • Headache, Tachycardia after large systemic doses

       • Beta 2 selective agonists are long acting or short:
          • Salbutamol (Ventolin) (short acting rescue remedy)
          • Salmeterol (long acting, take with inhaled corticosteroid)




39




                                                                                              13
                                                                                         17/06/2025




                               Bronchodilators
     • Anticholinergics/ antimuscarinic (against the parasympathetic
       nervous system’s main neurotransmitter, acetylcholine)
       • Block acetylcholine (muscarinic) receptors responsible for causing
         bronchoconstriction: allows the SNS to dominate
          • Bronchodilation
       • Adverse effects
          • Anticholinergic effects
              • Dry mouth, dizziness
       • Ipratropium bromide (Atrovent) (inhaler, nebuliser)
       • Interactions
          • Other anticholinergic drugs




40




             Leukotriene receptor antagonists
     • Leukotrienes are inflammatory mediators
     • Oral dose (PO; (means PER OS; Oral)) normally in addition to
       inhaled corticosteroids
     • Antagonists
       • Block leukotriene action
          • Reduced inflammatory response
          • Reduction in oedema, mucus secretion, smooth muscle contraction
       • Contraindication
          • Liver impairment (metabolised by the liver)
          • Pregnancy
       • Adverse effects:
          • Diarrhoea, nausea (Absorbed rapidly in the GI tract)
       • Zafirlukast (Accolate) and Montelukast


41




                                Corticosteroids
     Inhaled corticosteroids
     • Should be taken when the asthmatic starts reaching for their
       rescue inhaler more than 3 times per week
       • Decrease the inflammatory response
          • Prevent the rupture of mast cells > decreased chemical mediator response >
            reduced inflammatory and immune response = reduction in oedema,
            bronchoconstriction, mucus production = increased airflow
       • Adverse effects
          • Pharyngeal, laryngeal fungal infections (thrush)
       • Budesonide (Pulmicort)
       • Fluticasone (Seretide)




42




                                                                                                14
                                                                                      17/06/2025




                               Corticosteroids
     • Oral corticosteroids
       • Suppress immune and inflammatory response
          • Reducing the formation of prostaglandins and leukotrienes (required for
            inflammatory response)
          • Inhibit and impair the function of WBCs
       • Adverse effects
          • Infection, hyperglycaemia
       • Prednisone (PO)

       • Discussed in the endocrine system




43

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide4.xml
When you add up all the surface area for diffusion it is between
 the size of a tennis court and a football field. 
One of the important things to consider when we are trying to understand about pulmonary embolisms versus blockage of blood to other portions of the body is that the lungs have two sources of blood. One source is the pulmonary circulation, and that is the deoxygenated blood coming from the right side of the heart. But we also have the circulation that takes fresh blood from the left side of the heart and supplies the oxygen to the cells. A person with a small pulmonary embolism for instance, will have the flow of the pulmonary artery blocked, but will still be getting fresh blood to the cells for their oxygen and energy needs, so there will not be as much damage to the cellular structures, as long as the problem is resolved within a few months. 
8

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide5.xml
9
One of the important things to consider when we are trying to understand about pulmonary embolisms versus blockage of blood to other portions of the body is that the lungs have two sources of blood. One source is the pulmonary circulation, and that is the deoxygenated blood coming from the right side of the heart. But we also have the circulation that takes fresh blood from the left side of the heart and supplies the oxygen to the cells. A person with a small pulmonary embolism for instance, will have the flow of the pulmonary artery blocked, but will still be getting fresh blood to the cells for their oxygen and energy needs, so there will not be as much damage to the cellular structures, as long as the problem is resolved within a few months. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide6.xml
In this picture and associated diagram, the apices of the lungs are pictured
 just about right – possibly a bit lower than they should be. Compare the drawing to the X-ray and you would find that there is a bit of the lung missing from the drawing. 
The drawing over this young man depicts that pleural space which is well endowed with pain receptors and can fill with fluid from a number of problems. This would be called pleural effusion if not infected or 
pleuritis
 if there is an associated infection. Fluid from the pleural space does not drain into the lungs. It is taken out of the lungs by the lymphatic system. An infection in the pleural space just like an infection in the meninges, the pericardium, or the peritoneum is likely to be or turn into a clinical emergency for a number of reasons. The bacteria proliferate rapidly in these areas and the toxins can are transported all through the system very quickly. 
I would like to reiterate the fact that 
pleuritis
 is the same as pleurisy, which is the same thing as an infection in the pleural cavity, which is the same thing as an infected pleural effusion. The space there is separate from the alveoli. If the alveoli fill up with fluid, it is called pulmonary oedema or if infected, pneumonia. 
10

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide7.xml
11
Ventilation is 
bulk flow of air. You ventilate a room by opening a window. If the breeze remains constant the ventilation increases the larger the window gets. 
Obstructive disorders decrease ventilation, like closing the window. Obstructive disorders include asthma which decreases the diameter of the lumen of the airways because of the constriction of the airways and the excess mucus. 
Obstructive disorders include chronic bronchitis which decreases airway diameter because of the remodeling that occurs due to chronic insult. 
Obstructive disorders include emphysema which decreases our ability to exhale
So obstructive disorders decrease our ability to get air in and or out of the lungs. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide8.xml
12
Ventilation is 
bulk flow of air. You ventilate a room by opening a window. If the breeze remains constant the ventilation increases the larger the window gets. 
Obstructive disorders decrease ventilation, like closing the window. Obstructive disorders include asthma which decreases the diameter of the lumen of the airways because of the constriction of the airways and the excess mucus. 
Obstructive disorders include chronic bronchitis which decreases airway diameter because of the remodeling that occurs due to chronic insult. 
Obstructive disorders include emphysema which decreases our ability to exhale
So obstructive disorders decrease our ability to get air in and or out of the lungs. 

## ppt/notesSlides/notesSlide9.xml
13
External Respiration is the other function of the respiratory system.
 Respiration originally meant the 
process by which organisms exchange gases, especially oxygen and carbon dioxide, with the environment. The term respiration is now becoming used to explain both functions of the lungs (ventilation
 and external respiration) or even confused with ventilation. So perhaps we should start using the term gas exchange. 
The main factors in gas exchange include the distance for diffusion of gases and a decreased surface area for gas exchange
For instance, the reason that a large pulmonary embolism or multiple small pulmonary emboli are problematic is because there is less surface area available for gas exchange. 
Some of the occupational lung disorders adversely affect gas exchange because they make the surface area thicker, which decreases gas exchange

Medications of the Respiratory System:
Lesson 3
Decongestants and Antihistamines

## ppt/slides/slide21.xml
In allergic rhinitis, histamine dilates the blood vessels of the sinuses.
Decongestants constrict those blood vessels by acting on the alpha 1 adrenergic receptors.

## ppt/slides/slide22.xml
The sinuses
Normally, the sinuses are empty except for a thin layer of mucus.
We probably have sinuses to humidify the air

## ppt/slides/slide23.xml
Decongestants 
Decongestants
Help stop ‘runny nose’
Alpha agonists (act like adrenaline on the alpha 1 receptors)
Vasoconstriction = decrease  in secretions, reduction in swelling of mucus membranes and oedema , opening of nasal passage
Constrict the blood vessels that had been dilated by histamine

## ppt/slides/slide24.xml
Decongestants 
Nasal sprays
Oxymetazoline (
Drixine
)
Oral medications
Pseudoephedrine (sale is restricted)
Phenylephrine (no better than placebo in most trials)
Adverse effects
Rebound congestion (!: Don’t use long-term)
Hypertension (other blood vessels are constricted)
Insomnia 

## ppt/slides/slide25.xml
Antihistamines (H1 antagonists) 
Discussed in the tutorial
H1 antagonists (
NOT the H2 antagonists in gut
)
Help stop this entire cascade

## ppt/slides/slide26.xml
Antihistamines (H1 antagonists) 
Discussed in the tutorial
H1 are first generation (cross BBB; Dimenhydrinate)
Side effects include drowsiness and decreased motion sickness
These are often used for their ‘side effects’
These are associated with higher risk of Alzheimer’s disease 
H1 can be second generation 
do not cross BBB to the same degree, so less drowsiness
Loratadine and fexofenadine
Decrease the vasodilation effects of histamine
Antihistamines have varying amounts of secondary antimuscarinic actions 
This is usually more profound with the first generation medications: dry mouth, difficulty urinating, eye pain

The histamine receptors

th

## ppt/slides/slide31.xml
Asthma
A condition characterised by:
Bronchospasm
Inflammation
Hyperactive airways
Mucus hyper secretion
Oedema
Extrinsic
Allergens
Intrinsic
Non-allergic: anxiety, exercise
It may be best to consider an asthmatic to have a cup. Everything that irritates THEIR asthma fills up the cup. When the cup overflows, they have an asthmatic attack. 
Try to get them to eliminate anything that contributes to their asthma.

## ppt/slides/slide36.xml
Treatment of Asthma

efficacy is questioned
)

## ppt/slides/slide37.xml
Antihistamines in asthma
Antihistamines
Block the release of histamine
Reduces one mediator in the inflammatory and allergic response
Limited use in asthma because the mast cell that releases histamine also releases other stronger mediators of inflammation and the asthmatic is normally more sensitive to those.    But some asthmatics respond. 
 
 Adverse effects mainly seen with first generation 
antihistimines
 (the ones that cause drowsiness)
Anticholinergic effects if using first generation: don’t!
Dry mouth and drowsiness if using first generation: don’t!
CNS depressants if using first generation: don’t! 
Use second generation antihistamines: Loratadine and fexofenadine and discontinue if no effect. 

## ppt/slides/slide38.xml
Bronchodilators: inhaled
Dilate the airways, either mimicking the SNS (fight or flight) or blocking the PNS (rest and digest) in lungs

## ppt/slides/slide39.xml
Bronchodilators: inhaled
Beta
 2
 agonist (mimics the adrenaline on the Beta
 2
 receptor on lungs)
Beta
 2
 receptors are located on the smooth muscle of blood vessels, bronchi 
and MAST cells
Adverse effects
Headache, Tachycardia after large systemic doses
Beta
 2
 selective agonists are long acting or short:
Salbutamol (Ventolin) (short acting rescue remedy)
Sal
meter
ol
 (long acting, take with inhaled corticosteroid)

## ppt/slides/slide40.xml
Bronchodilators
Anti
chol
inergics/ anti
muscarinic
 (against the parasympathetic nervous system’s main neurotransmitter, acetylcholine)
Block acetylcholine (muscarinic) receptors responsible for causing bronchoconstriction: allows the SNS to dominate
Bronchodilation
Adverse effects
Anticholinergic effects
Dry mouth, dizziness
Ipratropium bromide (Atrovent) (inhaler, nebuliser)
Interactions
Other anticholinergic drugs

## ppt/slides/slide41.xml
Leukotriene receptor antagonists
Leukotrienes are inflammatory mediators
Oral dose (PO; (means PER OS; Oral)) normally in addition to inhaled corticosteroids
Antagonists
Block leukotriene action
Reduced inflammatory response
Reduction in oedema, mucus secretion, smooth muscle contraction 
Contraindication
Liver impairment (metabolised by the liver)
Pregnancy
Adverse effects:
 Diarrhoea, nausea (Absorbed rapidly in the GI tract)
Zafir
lukast
 (
Accolate
) and 
Monte
lukast

## ppt/slides/slide42.xml
Corticosteroids
Inhaled corticosteroids
Should be taken when the asthmatic starts reaching for their rescue inhaler more than 3 times per week
Decrease the inflammatory response
Prevent the rupture of mast cells > decreased chemical mediator response > reduced inflammatory and immune response = reduction in oedema, bronchoconstriction, mucus production = increased airflow
Adverse effects
Pharyngeal, laryngeal fungal infections (thrush)
Budesonide (
Pulmicort
)
Fluticasone (
Seretide
)

## ppt/slides/slide43.xml
Corticosteroids
Oral corticosteroids 
Suppress immune and inflammatory response
Reducing the formation of prostaglandins and leukotrienes (required for inflammatory response)
Inhibit and impair the function of WBCs 
Adverse effects
Infection, hyperglycaemia
Prednisone (PO)
Discussed in the endocrine system

===== Preview of respiratory system [_7758225_1] =====

===== Preview of respiratory system [_7758428_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758225_1?view=content&state=view
Review of the respiratory system
This lesson reviews the important points about the respiratory system that will help you understand the relevant medications.
The respiratory system is broadly divided into the upper and lower respiratory system. Upper respiratory system disorders that are treatable with medications include allergic rhinitis and the common cold, and our main drugs used to treat these include the antihistamines and the decongestants.
The lower respiratory system disorders that are treatable with our medications include those disorders that result in obstruction of the airways, which are sometimes referred to as the ventilation disorders. Common medications used to treat those disorders include bronchodilators, inhaled corticosteroids and oral corticosteroids. If the disorder results in thick or copious amounts of mucus, the expectorants or mucolytic agents may also be of value. We also have antitussives or cough medicines that work at the level of the medulla to decrease the urge to cough, but those should only be used for non-productive coughs.
There are several other disorders that involve the respiratory system, including pleural effusions, and pulmonary embolism, however those disorders are covered elsewhere, according to the origin of the complaint.
Video: Respiratory system review
This is an applied review, that includes the information about respiratory system disorders that you need to know in order to understand the drugs of the respiratory system. If you think you already know enough about the respiratory system that a 'review' will not be needed, watch the video anyway, and turn it up to 2X speed. (After the video starts, click onto the 1X symbol a couple times till it reads 2X, which is quite fast.)
YouTube video link

[437.98] And then where does it go? The only place it can go is up into the right side of the heart, and then be pushed out into the lungs.

[453.42] pulmonary embolism versus a blockage of blood to other portions of the body because of,
[460.98] for instance, a break off of of a blood clot is that the lungs have two sources of blood.
[468.42] One is the pulmonary circulation, and that's the deoxygenated blood.
[474.62] It comes right from the right side of the heart into the lungs.
[479.62] And that blood is what we're going to be taking over there to get oxygenated.
[485.98] The blood is already what we say spent. So it's it's already given up most of its oxygen, and it's going over to the lungs to get re oxygenated.
[496.37] And that is where the pulmonary embolism actually winds up.
[502.89] We also have another circulation that takes fresh blood from the left side of the heart.
[508.61] And it supplies the oxygen to the cells.
[513.29] So really when a person has a small pulmonary embolism, for instance, it will affect the flow of a small portion of the pulmonary artery.
[525.21] But the fresh blood to that same region is still going to be getting to those cells.
[531.81] So in a pulmonary embolism,
[534.09] there wouldn't be as much immediate damage to the cellular structures as long as the problem is resolved within a few months.
[545.77] Pulmonary emboli are treated with anticoagulants and they're also treated with fibre analytic drugs.
[553.57] And we'll discuss those as we get to the cardiovascular system.
[559.64] So to conclude this introduction to the respiratory system.
[563.72] Aside from the medications that will be kind of studying in different systems,
[570.40] our medications for the respiratory system disorders can help in a number of ways.
[575.68] And those that we just mentioned, but also in fighting infections, in decreasing cough, in preventing mucus formation,
[585.76] or in breaking up the mucus if it's really thick,
[590.32] in increasing the diameter of the airways to promote ventilation or bulk flow, and also by reducing inflammation.
[601.48] So that's what we're going to get to in this portion of the module.

===== Antitussives expectorants and mucolytic agents [_7758226_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758226_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Antitussives expectorants and mucolytic agents

===== Antitussives expectorants and mucolytic agents [_7758429_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758226_1?view=content&state=view
Overview of antitussives, expectorants and mucolytic agents
Although they often do not require a prescription, antitussives, expectorants and mucolytic agents are all widely used, and as such, very important to understand.
Antitussives (or cough suppressants) that are correctly named will work at the level of the medulla, and include the opioids and dextromethorphan. Other medications that soothe the lungs, or get mucus off the cough receptors are not termed antitussives.
Expectorants and mucolytic agents generally aim to do the same thing, to get mucus out of the lungs, but they work by different mechanisms. In this lesson, we explore these three important classes of medications in much more depth.
Video: Antitussives, expectorants and mucolytic agents
Most of the drugs discussed in this lesson are over the counter, but SOOO important to understand for everyone from a pharmacy assistant to an MD. Watch the video and then test your knowledge with the other activities.

--- h5p_a06f3c51aa591eb07721 ---
Any of the narcotic analgesics (opioids) like codeine, morphine, tramadol, or oxycodone would be a very effective cough suppressant, but unless strong pain is also an issue, they would not be the preferred therapy for relieving cough.

Expectorants like guaifenesin are good cough suppressants.

Antitussives can be used for productive coughs

Antitussives work at the level of the medulla

Dextromethorphan is an opioid

Dextromethorphan is a pregnancy category A drug and codeine is a pregnancy category C drug. As such Dextromethorphan is the preferred medication for treating a non-productive cough during pregnancy.

===== Decongestants and antihistamines [_7758227_1] =====

===== Decongestants and antihistamines [_7758430_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758227_1?view=content&state=view
The role of decongestants and antihistamines
When an immune response happens in the sinuses, histamine is released from the local mast cells, and the histamine dilates the blood vessels and increases the permeability of those blood vessels. The escaping fluid and cells work on the mucus secreting cells, resulting in both plasma and mucus being excreted. An antihistamine blocks the action of histamine on the receptors and a decongestant does the opposite thing that histamine does, contracting the same blood vessels that histamine dilated.
In this lesson, we concentrate on understanding that process and on the decongestants, as our tutorial for this week fully covers the antihistamines.
Video: Decongestants and antihistamines
Watch this video and engage in the M/C and T/F questions.

Common side effects associated with pseudoephedrine therapy include central nervous system stimulation, insomnia, nervousness, excitability, dizziness and anxiety. To offset this, the night time version of cold and flu medications including pseudoephedrine often include a first generation antihistamine, as those make a person feel drowsy.

===== Lower respiratory system disorders [_7758228_1] =====

Children:
Lower respiratory system disorders

===== Lower respiratory system disorders [_7758431_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758228_1?view=content&state=view
Overview of the lower respiratory system
The lower respiratory system disorders that are able to be treated by medications fall under the classification of the ventilation disorders, or in other words, the ones that obstruct the airways. Asthma is the most common of these, affecting over 10% of Australians. Since it is the most common and because of the fact that the medications used to treat asthma are often the ones that treat other lower respiratory system disorders, we aim to get a good picture of these in this lesson.
Video: Lower respiratory system disorders
Asthma is the most common lower respiratory system disorder in Australia. Learn the most important facts about the disorder by watching this video and engaging in the associated activities.

--- h5p_85e667ad238cd9dd68d1 ---
Asthma involves smooth muscle hypertrophy and hyperplasia, thickened basement membrane in the bronchi, hyperplasia of mucus glands, and can potentially result in thick mucus that dries, causing mucus plugs.

When the diameter of an airway decreases to half of its original size, the flow decreases to 16th meaning that a very small change in the diameter has a huge effect on the resistance of the airway.

Asthma is often called a reversible disorder, but if left untreated, it is likely to result in permanent changes in the airways that restricts the flow of air.

The best way to help asthmatic clients is to work extensively with one asthmatic client to find out what suggestions are helpful, and apply the same suggestions to all asthmatics.

Treatment of asthma initially includes the occasional use of inhaled short acting bronchodilators, and if those need to be taken more than four times per week, there will be an inclusion of a low-dose inhaled corticosteroid or other anti-inflammatory agent. More severe asthma includes stronger inhaled corticosteroids and finally the addition of oral corticosteroid treatment.

Asthma, Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD; the combination of chronic bronchitis and emphysema) and cystic fibrosis can be called obstructive or ventilation disorders, as they all involve obstruction of the airways.

Untitled Multiple Choice

===== Treatment of Asthma (and other Ventilation Disorders) [_7758229_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758229_1?view=content&state=view

Children:
Treatment of Asthma (and other Ventilation Disorders)

===== Treatment of Asthma (and other Ventilation Disorders) [_7758432_1] =====
Source: https://learn.scu.edu.au/ultra/courses/_156105_1/document/_7758229_1?view=content&state=view
Pharmacological management of asthma
As you have learned in the previous lessons, when an person experiences an asthmatic attack, the mast cells in their lungs degranulate and release histamine. So you would think that antihistamines are going to be helpful for asthmatics, but only about 10% of asthmatics get any additional benefits from them, and they should never be relied on for an acute asthmatic reaction. The main drugs for asthma are the bronchodilators, and those can be sympathomimetics, parasympatholytics, or other substances that dilate the lungs. Another very important target of our medications will be drugs that target the actual mediators of inflammation. The leukotrienes are mediators of inflammation that are often targeted, but the most useful medication that targets the immune system will be the corticosteroids (either inhaled or systemic). Corticosteroids decrease all arms of the immune system, so they are very important in the treatment of asthma.
Video: Treatment of Asthma
Remember that although asthma is regarded by many authors as a reversible disorder, it does involve chronic inflammation, and chronic inflammation almost always results in fibrosis and scarring, remodelling of the tissues, and permanent changes, so asthma must be treated and you must know how it is treated because 30% of Australians will need these asthma meds during at least one period of their lives.
